On a gap outside its transition strips, the reverse triangle inequality gives $$|B_N+R_N|\ge A-b=\frac38.$$ To check a transition strip at $z$, compare the phases after subtracting their values at $z$. Since $\phi_j'(z)=\psi_z'(z)$ and both derivatives are Lipschitz near $z$, $$\bigl|\phi_j(t)-\phi_j(z)-\psi_z(t)+\psi_z(z)\bigr|
       \le C_\delta|t-z|^2.$$ Together with <a href="#eq:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gamma-bounds">[eq:gamma-bounds]</a> and the endpoint phase agreement, this shows that the two phases differ, modulo an integer, by at most <a id="eq:phase-strip-error"></a>$$\label{eq:phase-strip-error}
 C_\delta(Nw^2+w)=O_\delta(N^{-1/2}).$$ Consequently, throughout that strip, $$B_N(t)+R_N(t)
 =\mathrm e\bigl(\alpha_z+N\psi_z(t)\bigr)
       \bigl(B(t)+A r_{j,N}(t)+O_\delta(N^{-1/2})\bigr).$$ Because $B(z)=b$ and $B$ is fixed and smooth, $B(t)\ge b-O_\delta(w)$ there. This proves the lower bound $b-o(1)$ on every strip. Off the gaps, $R_N=0$ and $B\ge b$, and reflection preserves all these modulus bounds. The upper bound is simpler: on gaps $|B_N+R_N|\le b+A=5/8<K_\delta$, and elsewhere it is at most $K_\delta$. This proves <a href="#eq:corrected-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:corrected-envelope">[eq:corrected-envelope]</a>.

The correction now gives the required lower envelope. The remaining task is to show that its Fourier coefficients cost only $O(\sqrt\delta)$ on the scale $N^{-1/2}$ and that discarding frequencies outside $\{0,\ldots,N-1\}$ changes it uniformly by $o(1)$.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### A bound for each Fourier coefficient

<a id="eq:piece-integral"></a>
<a id="eq:reflected-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:amplitude-bv"></a>
Split the integral for $\widehat R_N(k)$ into the three pieces of every gap and their reflections. On a positive piece its contribution has the form $$\label{eq:piece-integral}
 \int_I a(t)\mathrm e\bigl(N\phi(t)-kt\bigr)\,dt,
 \qquad a(t)=A r_{j,N}(t)\mathrm e\bigl(\gamma_{j,N}(t)\bigr),
 \quad \phi=\phi_j|_I.$$ On the reflected piece we use $\phi(t)=-\phi_j(-t)$ and $a(t)=\overline{A r_{j,N}(-t)\mathrm e(\gamma_{j,N}(-t))}$. In particular, the reflected phase derivative is $$\label{eq:reflected-derivative}
 \phi'(t)=\xi_j(-t).$$ Thus the original and reflected middle pieces use the same slot $D_j$. By <a href="#eq:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gamma-bounds">[eq:gamma-bounds]</a> and <a href="#eq:taper-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:taper-variation">[eq:taper-variation]</a>, on every piece $$\label{eq:amplitude-bv}
 \sup_I|a|+\int_I|a'(t)|\,dt
 \le A+2A+2\pi A=:C_1.$$ This constant is absolute, even if a gap is very short or there are many gaps.

<a id="eq:quadratic-integral"></a>
<a id="eq:first-derivative-integral"></a>
We use two elementary oscillatory integral estimates. If $\phi$ is quadratic with $\phi''=d\ne0$, then $$\label{eq:quadratic-integral}
 \left|\int_I a(t)\mathrm e\bigl(N\phi(t)-kt\bigr)\,dt\right|
 \le \frac{C}{\sqrt{N|d|}}
       \left(\sup_I|a|+\int_I|a'|\right).$$ Indeed, completing the square and scaling reduce the integral without $a$ over any subinterval to an integral of $\mathrm e(\pm u^2/2)$, multiplied by $(N|d|)^{-1/2}$. Those integrals are uniformly bounded: on $[-1,1]$ use length, and on each exterior half-line integrate by parts using the phase derivative $\pm u$. Integration against a primitive then proves <a href="#eq:quadratic-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-integral">[eq:quadratic-integral]</a>. We also use the first-derivative bound $$\label{eq:first-derivative-integral}
 \left|\int_I a(t)\mathrm e\bigl(N\phi(t)-kt\bigr)\,dt\right|
 \le \frac{C}{N\rho}
       \left(\sup_I|a|+\int_I|a'|\right)$$ when $N\phi'-k$ is monotone and has absolute value at least $N\rho$ on $I$. One integration by parts proves this bound, since the total variation of $1/(N\phi'-k)$ is at most $2/(N\rho)$.

Let $C_2$ be the absolute constant obtained by combining <a href="#eq:amplitude-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:amplitude-bv">[eq:amplitude-bv]</a> with <a href="#eq:quadratic-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-integral">[eq:quadratic-integral]</a>. The outer pieces and their reflections contribute at most $$\frac{2C_2\sqrt\delta}{\sqrt N}$$ by <a href="#eq:outer-slope-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:outer-slope-budget">[eq:outer-slope-budget]</a>. For the middle pieces choose a fixed $\rho>0$ so that the closed $\rho$-neighborhoods of the finitely many slots $D_j$ are disjoint. For any $k$, at most one slot is within distance $\rho$ of $k/N$. On that middle piece and its reflection, <a href="#eq:quadratic-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-integral">[eq:quadratic-integral]</a> and <a href="#eq:middle-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:middle-slope">[eq:middle-slope]</a> give a total bound $$\frac{4C_2\sqrt L}{\sqrt N}.$$ On all remaining middle pieces, $|N\phi'-k|\ge N\rho$, so <a href="#eq:first-derivative-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-derivative-integral">[eq:first-derivative-integral]</a> bounds their total contribution by $O_\delta(N^{-1})$, uniformly in $k$. The number of pieces and the separation $\rho$ enter only this last error. We conclude that $$\sqrt N\,|\widehat R_N(k)|
 \le 2C_2\sqrt\delta+4C_2\sqrt L+O_\delta(N^{-1/2}).$$ Since $L\le10\delta$, for sufficiently large $N$ this is at most $D\sqrt\delta$ with, for example, $D=2C_2+4\sqrt{10}C_2+1$. This proves <a href="#eq:correction-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-coefficients">[eq:correction-coefficients]</a> with an absolute constant. Hermitian symmetry also gives $$\overline{\widehat R_N(k)}
 =\int_\mathbb T\overline{R_N(t)}\mathrm e(kt)\,dt
 =\int_\mathbb TR_N(-t)\mathrm e(kt)\,dt
 =\widehat R_N(k).$$

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The Fourier mass outside the coefficient interval

<a id="eq:exterior-derivatives"></a>
<a id="eq:amplitude-two-derivatives"></a>
Let $k<0$ or $k\ge N$, and write $T=N+|k|$. On each piece in <a href="#eq:piece-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:piece-integral">[eq:piece-integral]</a>, set $$V(t)=N\phi'(t)-k.$$ The derivative containment <a href="#eq:derivative-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:derivative-interior">[eq:derivative-interior]</a>, also valid on reflected pieces by <a href="#eq:reflected-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reflected-derivative">[eq:reflected-derivative]</a>, implies $$\label{eq:exterior-derivatives}
 |V(t)|\ge c_\delta T,
 \qquad |V'(t)|\le C_\delta N,
 \qquad V''(t)=0$$ on each piece interior, after decreasing $c_\delta$ if necessary. The scaled smooth steps and the affine phase adjustments give $$\label{eq:amplitude-two-derivatives}
 \sup_I|a'|\le C_\delta w^{-1},
 \qquad \int_I|a''(t)|\,dt\le C_\delta w^{-1}.$$ For the second estimate, the term of size $w^{-2}$ from a differentiated step is supported on a strip of length $w$; the other terms are bounded using <a href="#eq:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gamma-bounds">[eq:gamma-bounds]</a>. Recall also the absolute bounds on $\sup|a|$ and $\int|a'|$ in <a href="#eq:amplitude-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:amplitude-bv">[eq:amplitude-bv]</a>.

<a id="eq:first-ibp-tail"></a>
One integration by parts on a piece gives $$\label{eq:first-ibp-tail}
 \int_I a\,\mathrm e(N\phi-kt)
 =\left[\frac{a\,\mathrm e(N\phi-kt)}{2\pi iV}\right]_{\partial I}
 -\frac1{2\pi i}\int_I(a/V)'\,\mathrm e(N\phi-kt).$$ The boundary terms in this first integration cancel when all pieces are summed. At a join within a gap, the numerator is the continuous function $R_N(t)\mathrm e(-kt)$ and the denominator agrees because $\phi_j'$ is continuous. At transition endpoints the numerator vanishes. At $0$, the reflected and original phase derivatives both equal $x_0$, and the numerator has the common value $A$. Finally, at the identified endpoints $-1/2$ and $1/2$, both phase derivatives equal $x_{1/2}$ and the numerator has the common value $A(-1)^k$. Thus cancellation holds on the full circle, including the two reflection joins.

For a second integration by parts define, on each piece, $$Q=\frac{(a/V)'}{V}
   =\frac{a'}{V^2}-\frac{aV'}{V^3}.$$ No cancellation of the new boundary terms is needed. Using $V''=0$, <a id="eq:second-ibp-algebra"></a>$$\label{eq:second-ibp-algebra}
 Q'=\frac{a''}{V^2}
       -\frac{3a'V'}{V^3}
       +\frac{3a(V')^2}{V^4}.$$ Equations <a href="#eq:exterior-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exterior-derivatives">[eq:exterior-derivatives]</a> and <a href="#eq:amplitude-two-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:amplitude-two-derivatives">[eq:amplitude-two-derivatives]</a> bound the endpoint values of $Q$ by $$C_\delta\left(\frac{w^{-1}}{T^2}+\frac{N}{T^3}\right)$$ and the integral of $|Q'|$ by $$C_\delta\left(\frac{w^{-1}}{T^2}
                 +\frac{N}{T^3}+\frac{N^2}{T^4}\right).$$ Summing over the fixed finite number of pieces in <a href="#eq:first-ibp-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-ibp-tail">[eq:first-ibp-tail]</a>, and integrating its remaining integrals once more, therefore gives $$|\widehat R_N(k)|
 \le C_\delta\left(\frac{w^{-1}}{T^2}
                 +\frac{N}{T^3}+\frac{N^2}{T^4}\right)
 \le \frac{C_\delta w^{-1}}{(N+|k|)^2}.$$ Since $\sum_{k<0\text{ or }k\ge N}(N+|k|)^{-2}=O(N^{-1})$, we obtain $$\sum_{k<0\text{ or }k\ge N}|\widehat R_N(k)|
       \le C_\delta(Nw)^{-1}=O_\delta(N^{-1/4}),$$ as asserted in <a href="#eq:correction-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-tail">[eq:correction-tail]</a>. This completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:correction">8</a>. $\square$

In particular, the Fourier series of $R_N$ is absolutely convergent. Its sum equals $R_N$: both are continuous and have the same Fourier coefficients, so uniqueness of Fourier coefficients applies. Hence <a id="eq:correction-projection"></a>$$\label{eq:correction-projection}
 \sup_{t\in\mathbb T}
 \left|R_N(t)-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\widehat R_N(k)\mathrm e(kt)\right|
       =O_\delta(N^{-1/4}).$$ We may therefore add the correction using only the required consecutive degrees, with a uniform error tending to zero.

<a id="sec:rounding"></a>
### Rounding with a small defect mass

The uniform rounding error bounds the loss in the lower modulus and the increase in the upper modulus. The following estimate controls that error in terms of the total distance of the relaxed coefficients from signs. We give the full argument from (OpenAI 2026, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[OpenAIMinimal](#ref-OpenAIMinimal)]. Its discrepancy input belongs to Spencer’s partial-coloring method (Spencer 1985) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Spencer1985](#ref-Spencer1985)]; the precise real-vector theorem used below is due to Lovett and Meka (Lovett and Meka 2015, Theorem 4 of arXiv:1203.5747v2) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[LovettMeka2015](#ref-LovettMeka2015)].

<a id="stmt-lem-rounding"></a>
<div id="lem:rounding" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Rounding with a small defect). *There is an absolute constant $C$ with the following property. Let $N\ge1$ be an integer and let $X=(X_0,\ldots,X_{N-1})\in[-1,1]^N$. Put $$\mu(X)=\frac12\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(1-|X_k|).$$ Then there are signs $\varepsilon_0,\ldots,\varepsilon_{N-1}$ satisfying <a id="eq:defect-rounding"></a>$$\label{eq:defect-rounding}
 \max_{t\in\mathbb T}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\varepsilon_k-X_k)\mathrm e(kt)\right|
 \le C\left(1+\sqrt{\mu(X)\log\frac{80N}{\mu(X)}}\right).$$ The square-root term is defined to be zero when $\mu(X)=0$. In that case one may take $\varepsilon=X$, giving zero error.*

</div>

We first obtain a discrepancy estimate for arbitrary real matrices. We then round the coefficient defects on successively coarser dyadic grids. At every scale a simultaneous reversal of the discrepancy signs keeps the total defect mass from increasing. This controls how many coordinates can move and yields the dependence on $\mu(X)$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### A discrepancy bound for real matrices

<a id="stmt-lem-rounding-matrix"></a>
<div id="lem:rounding-matrix" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *There is an absolute constant $C_0$ such that, for integers $1\le s\le R$ and a real matrix $B\in[-1,1]^{R\times s}$, there is a vector $\xi\in\{-1,1\}^s$ satisfying $$\left\lVert B\xi\right\rVert_\infty\le C_0\sqrt{s\log(2R/s)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rounding-matrix"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rounding-matrix</summary>

*Proof.* We use the following existential consequence of the Lovett–Meka theorem (Lovett and Meka 2015, Theorem 4 of arXiv:1203.5747v2) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[LovettMeka2015](#ref-LovettMeka2015)]. Given vectors $v_1,\ldots,v_R\in\mathbb R^u$, a starting point $x_0\in[-1,1]^u$, and nonnegative numbers $c_1,\ldots,c_R$ such that $$\sum_{i=1}^R\exp(-c_i^2/16)\le u/16,$$ there exists, for every sufficiently small $\eta>0$, a point $x\in[-1,1]^u$ with $$|\langle v_i,x-x_0\rangle|\le c_i\left\lVert v_i\right\rVert_2
 \quad(1\le i\le R),
 \qquad
 |x_k|\ge1-\eta\quad\text{for at least }u/2\text{ coordinates }k.$$ The cited theorem finds such a point with positive probability; we use only its existence assertion. Letting $\eta$ tend to zero and taking a convergent subsequence in the compact cube gives a point with at least $\lceil u/2\rceil$ coordinates equal to signs and the same discrepancy bounds. Indeed, there are only finitely many choices of these coordinates, and all the displayed inequalities are closed under limits.

Start at zero in $[-1,1]^s$. At a stage with $u$ coordinates not yet equal to signs, restrict the rows of $B$ to those coordinates, take their current values as $x_0$, and choose $$c_i=4\sqrt{\log(16R/u)}\qquad(1\le i\le R).$$ The threshold sum is exactly $u/16$, and every restricted row has Euclidean norm at most $\sqrt u$. Thus this step changes each row sum by at most $4\sqrt{u\log(16R/u)}$ and fixes at least half the remaining coordinates. Leave the fixed coordinates unchanged and repeat. Zero rows impose no restriction. The process terminates with a sign vector.

If $u_h$ is the number of remaining coordinates before step $h$, starting with $h=0$, then $u_h\le2^{-h}s$. The function $u\log(16R/u)$ is increasing for $0<u\le R$. With $L=\log(16R/s)\ge\log16$, the sum of the row errors is therefore at most $$\begin{aligned}
 4\sum_h\sqrt{u_h\log(16R/u_h)}
 &\le4\sqrt{sL}\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}2^{-h/2}
       \sqrt{1+\frac{h\log2}{L}}\\
 &\le4\sqrt{sL}\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}2^{-h/2}\sqrt{1+h/4}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The last series converges, and $\log(16R/s)\le4\log(2R/s)$ for $s\le R$. This proves the lemma with an absolute constant, including when $s=R$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Dyadic rounding and the Fourier error

Apply the matrix estimate to real and imaginary Fourier evaluations on a grid of $20N$ points. After dyadic rounding, a derivative bound for the error polynomial will transfer the estimate to the whole circle.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rounding-matrix-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rounding-matrix</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rounding">9</a>.* Let $X=(X_0,\ldots,X_{N-1})\in[-1,1]^N$ and $\mu=\frac12\sum_k(1-|X_k|)$. If $\mu=0$, every $X_k$ is already a sign, and we take $\varepsilon_k=X_k$. Suppose henceforth that $0<\mu\le N/2$. Set $$\sigma_k=\begin{cases}1,&X_k\ge0,\\-1,&X_k<0,\end{cases}
 \qquad p_k=\frac{1-|X_k|}{2}.$$ Then $X_k=\sigma_k(1-2p_k)$, $0\le p_k\le1/2$, and $\sum_kp_k=\mu$. In particular, this definition includes $X_k=0$.

Put $M=20N$ and $R=2M=40N$. For the grid points $t_\ell=\ell/M$, $0\le\ell<M$, define the real $R\times N$ matrix $A$ by $$A_{2\ell,k}=\sigma_k\cos(2\pi kt_\ell),\qquad
 A_{2\ell+1,k}=\sigma_k\sin(2\pi kt_\ell)
 \quad(0\le k<N).$$ Its entries belong to $[-1,1]$, and every selection of its columns has at most $N\le R$ columns.

<a id="eq:rounding-initial"></a>
<a id="eq:rounding-step"></a>
Choose an integer $J\ge1$ with $N2^{-J}\le1$, and first round down: $$p^{(J)}_k=2^{-J}\lfloor2^Jp_k\rfloor.$$ This does not increase the mass, and $$\label{eq:rounding-initial}
 \left\lVert A(p^{(J)}-p)\right\rVert_\infty
 \le\sum_k|p^{(J)}_k-p_k|\le N2^{-J}\le1.$$ We next construct $p^{(j-1)}$ from $p^{(j)}$, for $j=J,J-1,\ldots,1$. Intermediate entries may exceed $1/2$, but it is enough to keep them in $[0,1]$, whose endpoints encode the two choices of final sign. We preserve the two properties $$p^{(j)}\in([0,1]\cap2^{-j}\mathbb Z)^N,
 \qquad \sum_kp^{(j)}_k\le\mu.$$ Let $S_j$ be the set of coordinates for which $2^jp^{(j)}_k$ is odd, and let $s_j=|S_j|$. If $s_j=0$, no change is needed. Otherwise apply Lemma <a href="#lem:rounding-matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rounding-matrix">10</a> to the columns indexed by $S_j$, obtaining signs $\xi_k$. Replace all these signs by their negatives if necessary so that $\sum_{k\in S_j}\xi_k\le0$, and define $$p^{(j-1)}_k=
 \begin{cases}
 p^{(j)}_k+2^{-j}\xi_k,&k\in S_j,\\
 p^{(j)}_k,&k\notin S_j.
 \end{cases}$$ An odd integer in $[0,2^j]$ lies between $1$ and $2^j-1$; adding either sign therefore gives an even integer still in $[0,2^j]$. Thus the new vector belongs to $([0,1]\cap2^{-(j-1)}\mathbb Z)^N$. Its mass does not increase, and the simultaneous sign reversal has left the discrepancy unchanged. The step error is consequently at most $$\label{eq:rounding-step}
 \left\lVert A(p^{(j-1)}-p^{(j)})\right\rVert_\infty
 \le C_0\,2^{-j}\sqrt{s_j\log(80N/s_j)}
 \qquad(s_j>0).$$

<a id="eq:rounding-matrix-error"></a>
Every coordinate in $S_j$ is at least $2^{-j}$, so the mass bound gives $$s_j\le\min(N,2^j\mu).$$ This is where the total defect enters the estimate. Put $U_j=2^j\mu$ and $L_\mu=\log(80N/\mu)$. The function $f(s)=s\log(80N/s)$ is increasing on $(0,N]$. If $U_j\le N$, then $$f(s_j)\le f(U_j)
 =U_j\log(80N/U_j)\le U_jL_\mu.$$ If $U_j>N$, then, using $\mu\le N/2$, $$f(s_j)\le N\log80\le U_jL_\mu.$$ These estimates apply at every nonempty stage. Summing <a href="#eq:rounding-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-step">[eq:rounding-step]</a> and <a href="#eq:rounding-initial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-initial">[eq:rounding-initial]</a> therefore gives, for $p'=p^{(0)}\in\{0,1\}^N$, $$\label{eq:rounding-matrix-error}
 \begin{aligned}
 \left\lVert A(p'-p)\right\rVert_\infty
 &\le1+C_0\sqrt{\mu L_\mu}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j/2}\\
 &=1+\frac{C_0}{\sqrt2-1}\sqrt{\mu\log(80N/\mu)}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Empty stages contribute zero, without invoking the discrepancy bound with no columns.

<a id="eq:rounding-grid"></a>
Now set $\varepsilon_k=\sigma_k(1-2p'_k)\in\{-1,1\}$ and define the error polynomial $$Q(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\varepsilon_k-X_k)z^k
     =-2\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\sigma_k(p'_k-p_k)z^k.$$ By the definition of $A$, the real and imaginary parts of $Q(\mathrm e(t_\ell))$ each have absolute value at most $2\left\lVert A(p'-p)\right\rVert_\infty$. Hence $$\label{eq:rounding-grid}
 G:=\max_{0\le\ell<M}|Q(\mathrm e(t_\ell))|
 \le2\sqrt2\,\left\lVert A(p'-p)\right\rVert_\infty.$$ It remains to pass from this grid bound to the whole circle. We apply this step only to $Q$, the rounding error.

Write $S=\max_{|z|=1}|Q(z)|$. If $Q=0$, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise let $d\le N-1$ be its degree. Applying the maximum principle to $Q$ on the unit disk and to the reversed polynomial $z^dQ(1/z)$ gives $$|Q(z)|\le S\max(1,|z|^d),
 \qquad
 \max_{|z|\le1+1/N}|Q(z)|\le \exp(1)S.$$ Cauchy’s estimate on the disk of radius $1/N$ centered at a point of the unit circle yields $|Q'(z)|\le\exp(1)NS$ there. Consequently $$\left|\frac{d}{dt}Q(\mathrm e(t))\right|
 \le2\pi\exp(1)NS.$$ Every point of $\mathbb T$ has circular distance at most $1/(2M)$ from the grid. Integrating this derivative along the shorter arc gives $$S\le G+\frac{\pi\exp(1)}{20}S,
 \qquad
 S\le\frac{G}{1-\pi\exp(1)/20}.$$ The denominator is positive. Combining this inequality with <a href="#eq:rounding-grid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-grid">[eq:rounding-grid]</a> and <a href="#eq:rounding-matrix-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-matrix-error">[eq:rounding-matrix-error]</a> proves Lemma <a href="#lem:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rounding">9</a>, with an absolute constant. The same argument covers $N=1$, when the error polynomial is constant. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
### Proof of the main theorem

We now combine the construction with the rounding estimate. The order of the choices is important: the accuracy parameter fixes all auxiliary data, and only then does the length tend to infinity.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rounding-matrix-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rounding-matrix</summary>

<a id="eq:completion-envelope"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Let $b=1/8$ and let $D>0$ be the absolute constant in Proposition <a href="#prop:correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:correction">8</a>. For a fixed sufficiently small $\delta>0$, take the real coefficients $Y_{N,k}$ and the functions $B_N$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:starting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:starting">6</a>, and the correction $R_N$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:correction">8</a>. All estimates below hold for every sufficiently large integer $N$. Put $$s_\delta=D\sqrt\delta,\qquad S_\delta=1+s_\delta,
 \qquad
 X_{N,k}=\frac{Y_{N,k}+\sqrt N\,\widehat R_N(k)}{S_\delta}
 \quad(0\le k<N).$$ Conjugate symmetry of $R_N$ makes $\widehat R_N(k)$ real. Since $|Y_{N,k}|\le1$ and $\sqrt N|\widehat R_N(k)|\le s_\delta$, the vector $X_N$ belongs to $[-1,1]^N$. The uniform approximation in Proposition <a href="#prop:starting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:starting">6</a> and the exterior Fourier-tail estimate in Proposition <a href="#prop:correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:correction">8</a> give <a id="eq:completion-profile"></a>$$\label{eq:completion-profile}
 U_{X_N}(t)=\frac{B_N(t)+R_N(t)}{S_\delta}+o(1)
 \qquad\text{uniformly for }t\in\mathbb T.$$ Consequently, $$\label{eq:completion-envelope}
 \min_{t\in\mathbb T}|U_{X_N}(t)|\ge\frac b{S_\delta}-o(1),
 \qquad
 \left\lVert U_{X_N}\right\rVert_\infty\le K_\delta+o(1).$$ In the second inequality we used $S_\delta\ge1$.

<a id="eq:completion-rounding"></a>
The same coefficient bound controls the cost of rounding. Directly, $$|X_{N,k}-Y_{N,k}|
 =\frac{|\sqrt N\,\widehat R_N(k)-s_\delta Y_{N,k}|}{S_\delta}
 \le2s_\delta.$$ Both coefficients lie in $[-1,1]$, so $|X_{N,k}^2-Y_{N,k}^2|\le4s_\delta$. Using the mean-square estimate from Proposition <a href="#prop:starting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:starting">6</a> and $1-|x|\le1-x^2$ for $|x|\le1$, we obtain <a id="eq:completion-defect"></a>$$\label{eq:completion-defect}
 \frac{\mu(X_N)}N
 \le\frac1{2N}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(1-X_{N,k}^2)
 \le4\delta+2D\sqrt\delta=:q_\delta.$$ Choose $\delta$ small enough that $q_\delta<1/2$. The function $q\mapsto q\log(80/q)$ is increasing on $(0,1/2]$. Lemma <a href="#lem:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rounding">9</a> therefore supplies signs $\varepsilon_0,\ldots,\varepsilon_{N-1}$ with $$\label{eq:completion-rounding}
 \left\lVert U_\varepsilon-U_{X_N}\right\rVert_\infty
 \le \frac C{\sqrt N}
       +C\sqrt{q_\delta\log(80/q_\delta)}.$$ If $\mu(X_N)=0$, we instead take $\varepsilon_k=X_{N,k}$, so this bound also covers that case.

Fix $c=1/16$ once and for all. As $\delta\downarrow0$, $$\frac b{S_\delta}
       -C\sqrt{q_\delta\log(80/q_\delta)}\longrightarrow\frac18,
 \qquad
 K_\delta+C\sqrt{q_\delta\log(80/q_\delta)}\longrightarrow1.$$ For any prescribed $\eta>0$, first choose $\delta$ so small that the first expression is strictly larger than $c$ and the second is strictly smaller than $1+\eta$. Fix every construction datum associated with this $\delta$. We may then choose $N_0$ so that for every $N\ge N_0$ all preceding estimates hold and the errors in <a href="#eq:completion-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:completion-envelope">[eq:completion-envelope]</a> and <a href="#eq:completion-rounding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:completion-rounding">[eq:completion-rounding]</a> are smaller than these strict margins. It follows that $$c\le|U_\varepsilon(t)|\le1+\eta\qquad(t\in\mathbb T).$$ Multiplication by $\sqrt N$ gives the asserted polynomial. Rounding assigns a real sign to each of the consecutive indices $0,\ldots,N-1$; all estimates include $t=0$ and $t=1/2$ and hold through every sufficiently large integer length. ◻

</details>

      "caption": "Schematic of the correction. In (a), the leading phase derivative $ξ_{j} = ϕ_{j}′$ matches the prescribed endpoint values and traverses $D_{j}$ on the middle piece.
